ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e2310	Thank you. – First of all, a wonderful morning, Mr. Finanzminister, and now the question you have already put to you in writing has been read. Federal Minister! My question is: [[/PAKT/VHG/XX/M/M_00048/index.shtml -- 48/M]] What are the consequences of the Wifo redistribution report, according to which all institutions affected by self-employment in 1993 would have had to make around 1.7 billion schilling more deposits into the FLAF, would they not have been exempt from the service provider's contribution?
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e2340	Please, Minister of State.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e2355	Mr President. High house! Ladies and gentlemen! Ladies and gentlemen! In its redistribution study, the Wifo cites a work by Badelt, who notes that in 1993 all institutions affected by self-employment would have had to pay about 1.7 billion schilling more into the system if they had not been exempt from the service provider's contribution. Ladies and gentlemen! In concrete terms, the so-called self-carriers, i.e. the local authorities and non-profit hospitals, bear family and birth aid themselves. However, it is undisputed that, in so far as they are publicly funded, school-leaving, school-books and other services are also financed by FLAF for the employees of these so-called self-employed persons. For a long time now, the Federal Government's desire – it had already been taken into account in the BVA 1994 – has been to abolish this self-employment, but this of course has resulted in a vehement rejection of the countries and especially the communities, because it would have taxed them more financially. However, it is fair to say that this fact was also taken into account during the last financial compensatory negotiations in the overall distribution of the burdens and obligations and rights of the countries and municipalities and the federal government. That is to say, we have taken account of this fact that, from our point of view, a contribution by the self-carriers to FLAF itself is too low, and have therefore agreed with the countries and communities to make no change in the current legal situation by the year 2000 - that is the duration of the financial compensation.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e2714	Federal Minister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e2729	Ladies and gentlemen! That is, I think, a very fundamental question. Therefore, allow me to forward a few preliminary remarks to the reply. First preliminary remark: Austria, with its tax rate of about 43 percent internationally, is in the top third. This means that we have little room to find more additional revenue by means of taxes or tax increases or similar. The conclusion from it, ladies and gentlemen, must be that we are very, very intelligent in dealing with the existing sum of money that we collect from the taxpayers in Austria year after year. The conclusion must also be that we cannot afford to apply the principle of foundry, even if at times it would be easier. In other words, the conclusion must be: If we need to take social aspects into account in the expenditure of the State, we must in fact strive to achieve social staggering of transfers. This social staggering of transfers, ladies and gentlemen, is possible by, for example, increasing physical benefits, but also by increasing the reliability of Wifo's distribution study in certain areas, which is a very, very good indication, one can see that one really gives it to the people they need. From my point of view, there is a large area of application. We already have this social staggering in many areas today, for example in the area of study aid, which depends on income, in the area of housing promotion – but there only very rudimentary, because there are only a few countries that actually rely largely on the subject promotion, that is to say, on the individual need for funding of the individual – and more. Of course, there is also a great deal of importance in this context – and this must be said quite clearly – of child support and similar issues. I must say quite clearly here that we must not violate the top principle of individual taxation. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! In Austrian tax law, it is quite clear that an employed woman is to be taxed as an independent individual and is not regarded as a tax liability or tax deduction of the husband. We don't want that either! Therefore, individual taxation is a principle that we must not compromise. [[Congratulations with representatives of the SPÖ.]] Ladies and gentlemen too may agree that we do not want to endanger individual taxation. [[Accompaniment with the SPÖ and the Liberal Forum and the Abg. Oils.]] Ladies and gentlemen! The second principle: I also say that in all openness, it has not succeeded in reaching a joint opinion from the Federal Government on a test procedure at the Constitutional Court, which takes into account the higher maintenance performance of a service provider by being able to write it off fully from its tax base, which would result in a massive redistribution towards the upper levels of income. We cannot and do not want to accept this because, according to this study, it is about supporting the poor. We therefore assume that the state ensures a certain minimum supply – for every citizen equal to – through the amount of child drop-out and child support – that we do not want to violate this principle, but that certain additional claims from children of richer parents are covered by the higher incomes of parents. I believe that this type of allowance, i.e. children's allowance plus children's allowance, is, in principle, a very reasonable system, which we should not change, but that social staggering of transfers is an indisputable necessity if we are to admit that the State's financial resources are limited.

[[Call to the liberals: We too!]] Ladies and gentlemen, Mr Koppler! Allow me to make two preliminary remarks. The first: I'm really not a dumped nationalist and chauvinist. Second preliminary remark: We know that enemy take-overs usually result in very fast-pacing important functions and therefore important work for service sectors, for industry – which starts with legal advice – going through financing, insurance business to development, research, central marketing – to foreign centres. This is the reason for such business concentrations. In the context of the gradual establishment of a level of personnel within the Federal Armed Forces, there has been a substantial intensification of stripping activity and we are continuing to build up the personnel level. It should also be noted that these activities have already led to a marked decline in illegal border crossings, such as how many illegals are still being attacked at the German border, and in the half-yearly comparison between 1995 and 1996, compared with the first half of each year, a reduction of the illegals by about 35 percent. [[Intervocations with the liberals.]] It can also be noted that there is a marked decline in the burden of crime, particularly in the nearby districts. The other part of the answer is that we do not primarily need a suspicious control. In order to say that quite clearly: Our aim cannot be to open the borders on the one hand, and on the other hand to suspend the Austrians intensive control by police, Gendarmerie and customs guards on the other. Our goal is freedom of movement and not an intensification of the control and restriction of civil liberties. But our objective is, of course, to fight effectively both cross-border crime and illegal migration. We therefore have a concept which essentially provides that, in the context of intensified criminal police activity – and this may be suspicious – appropriate control will take place, in particular at the southern border, if the borders between Austria and Italy are to be removed in the context of the implementation of the Schengen Convention by both countries.

This leads, on average, to a rather good control of the Green Border when it comes to a little over 1,500 men. The division of work between the members of the Austrian Federal Army and the members of the executive is such that the staff of the executive from the moment when it comes to an attack, take over, hear and continue to treat the persons taken over, both as regards administrative activities, and as regards the immediate activities, i.e. transfer to appropriate places and so on, so that the discharge of the executive is not as complete as it may occasionally appear. We have the impression, on the other hand, that it is a very difficult burden for presenters to carry out this service, because it is because it shows that these mostly 19-year-old men are away from home for the first time and suffer very much from loneliness – so strangely this may sound because one should think that at the age of 19 they could actually have grown up quite a bit. This actually leads to major problems there and there, which should not be underestimated. Secondly, we are firmly convinced that the task of border control is not a military task but a civilian task. It is therefore our aim to make preparations in such a way that, at the end of the legislative period, the area of the border between Burgenland and those four to five kilometres on the Lower Austrian border, at which the German army carries out control tasks today, can be fully carried out by members of the executive. It is also my aim, in talks with the Federal Minister for Defence, to gradually change the weighting, because we believe that assistance can easily take over and deliver substantial achievements, for example in the field of logistics, where the executive is in fact difficult to achieve cost-effectively and in such a way that Gendarmerie officials do not spend hours and days in Austria in order to transport for instance displaced persons but, on the other hand, gradually to take over border control by professional forces of executives. However, the use of assistance, even in my view, remains consistent until the end of the legislative period.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e4968	Mr President. High house! Oils.]] Instead of arguments, we have really only heard polemics here. I don't want to go into this style anymore. We have undertaken that my colleagues will refer to specific areas of this distribution report. I would therefore like to focus on some political conclusions. First point: I think this distribution report shows that there is an effective aid for lower incomes in total Austria. Of course, the redistribution in this country is limited, but it is effective. Second point: This redistribution is mainly carried out through the expenditure side rather than through the revenue side of the public sector, and that is, of course, a consolidation policy, which would focus only on the expenditure side, would necessarily have negative effects on lower incomes. That is why it is true that the consolidation package, as we have decided, is designed in such a way that it includes both sides, both the expenditure side and the revenue side. Third point: If we look at the tax side in detail, we have negative effects on the distribution of indirect taxes and social security contributions, which are only slightly offset by the effect of progressive income taxation and capital income tax. And this, Mr Böhacker, is what Dr. Guger meant by the quote you referred to. One has to say clearly here: This is a development that we must pay attention to, because, of course, there is something like an international tax competition which leads to the most mobile factors, such as the capital factor, being favoured at the expense of the less mobile factor labour and therefore to an ever greater tax burden on labour. It has only recently been presented by Commissioner Monti by the EU Commission that shows that between 1980 and 1994, the burden of taxes on labour increased from 34.7 to 40.5% of the total wage, while the burden on capital has fallen from 44.1 to 35.2 percent. This means that we have a disparate development here, and the most important way to deal with it is through international coordination, i.e. tax coordination instead of genuinely harmful tax competition. I think that this is one of the most important concerns we have to achieve in the EU, and I would also like to say that it is an issue that is directly linked to European monetary union. Next point: If we look at the expenditure page, I think it is certainly so that we need to differentiate there. There are areas of expenditure which benefit mainly lower incomes, for example the whole area of social expenditure, and there are areas of expenditure which benefit mainly or to a large extent higher incomes. And I think that is the area in which it is actually useful to make social differentiations. All in all, ladies and gentlemen, this distribution report shows that in Austria we have a system in which the public sector works in order to ensure lower incomes, in the sense of safeguarding the weak and also in the sense of safeguarding against life's changes. I believe that in this sense we should see this distribution report as an incentive to carry on precisely these tasks of a functioning social state in the future.

First of all, ladies and gentlemen: I believe that the humanity of a society is measured by how it deals with the socially weak. We from the EIA are committed to social solidarity within the meaning of the regulatory model of the social market economy. For us social solidarity means that the strong social and solidarity benefits help those who are socially weak and unable to provide a living for themselves. – This is social solidarity. Ladies and gentlemen! However, I would like to draw a very clear line between social solidarity in the sense of the social market economy – social solidarity is an integral part of this model of order – and redistribution policy as an instrument of Marxist social philosophy and socialist social policy, which is simply about taking away as much of the individual as possible in order to have as large a redistributive mass as possible. To this end, we have to say very clearly: This is an important social policy issue, namely the question of: What should be the scope of the individual's decision on the use of his employment income? Should the individual have as much scope as possible for deciding how to use his work income, or should the society, the state by means of taxes, contributions and taxes take away as much as possible in order to redistribut as possible? Ladies and gentlemen! We from the People's Party give a clear rejection to the distribution policy à la Marx, but we say a clear yes to social solidarity in the sense of the social market economy. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! In this context, I think one has to say very clearly, and I am familiar with this: It is not possible to define social needs once and for all. Social needs are constantly changing as a consequence of economic, social, technical development. But if one says that social needs cannot be determined once and for all, then this has two consequences: First consequence – and I say this very clearly now –: You can never cry out a social stop because new social needs arise again and again. The second consequence, however, is: Once introduced social benefits are not to be taboo for all time, but they must be constantly adapted to social needs, they must be transformed into the social state, and I believe that it is one of the major challenges for our generation to ensure this system of social security in the long term. Ladies and gentlemen! If there is any need to have a redistribution discussion, one thing is also very clear: For me, employment policy is still a priority, and we must be very careful to ensure that the site of Austria is safeguarded at work. I do not want to have a state in which we have a beautiful social system on the paper, but no more jobs to finance this social system. – You have to say that very clearly, I think. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Ladies and gentlemen! If one reads the distribution report carefully, one can see that it is written that, for example, in Switzerland – where neither Karl Marx nor his epigons are known to be in power – there is a greater progressive tax revenue than in us that, on average, each country takes 5.5 percent of its wealth taxes while in Austria we have abolished property tax. [[Interlocutory calls to the liberals]] Therefore, I do not believe that we should call any correction "dramatic class struggle" on the basis of the distribution report. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] We should be concerned that the wage rate has fallen by 3 percent since the end of the 1980s. It has always been assumed that, if profits are in order, they will also be properly invested. The distribution report tells us: The profits rise, they are fine – yet the investments fall. – We will have to discuss this in the House. The distribution report clearly states: From the revenue side of the State, there is essentially no progression effect. – We can talk about whether we want to have a progression effect, what opportunities there are in this context. I think this is entirely legitimate. At least one should discuss whether one can eliminate the maximum contribution base and obtain a volume of distribution resulting in a stronger progression. We can also discuss how to proceed on the issue of taxation of assets and capital. There is a very interesting approach here in the distribution report concerning real estate. Clearly, real estate is not subject to the same escape component as mobile capital – something that is clearly observed in France, Holland and Switzerland, for example. That is to say, there are already ideas in the distribution report that are not attributed to astronomy and moonlight – as Professor Lukesch says – but are already practiced in other civilized Western European states. We should consider whether we cannot learn something from these experiences for ourselves. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] If we look at the issue of the expenditure side – it has a certain degree of redistribution – we must find that there is already a positive response to budget consolidation. The distribution report says quite precisely: The main beneficiaries of interest payments for government debt are two-thirds of those in the upper-income third. That means: If the Republic decides to reduce the budget deficit and thereby also the interest payments, we will at least be able to reduce this negative distribution effect in the future. As regards the second area, health expenditure, which has already been addressed today, the distribution report also states: Self-retaining in the health sector would primarily affect the lower incomes of the population. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Is this desirable, or should we not rather discuss the free co-insurance of spouses or the withdrawal of maximum contributions in this context, which could lead to a different distribution effect? We should also discuss the accuracy of their objectives in terms of housing promotion – I believe that this is highly appropriate, even if it is a matter of national importance. 35 billion schillings are spent per anno, but we must note that the distribution situation is wrong, namely that the lower levels of income are insufficient, but the middle and higher levels of income are relatively higher. I believe that this instrument of social compensation, which also serves to stimulate the construction sector, must be reviewed, and that the countries should not be closed to a discussion on how housing support could be adapted in the individual countries in the sense of greater social justice. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! I therefore believe, in summary, that the distribution report, if taken seriously and considered as a mirror of the material conditions of our society, cannot be a basis for any cheap polemics to be taken from the debate, but that this report should be taken as an occasion in areas where it is desirable and necessary to make corrections. We do not want to reach a state – in various books it is already sketched – similar to the United States or the United Kingdom, where today the biggest inequality has existed since there were statistics. This kind of society is deeply inhuman. To prevent them must be the goal of this House. The corresponding consequences of the distribution report should therefore be removed. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e7064	Mr Dkfm is speaking on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats). Ruthofer. Please, honourable Member. Voluntary speaking time limit: 6 minutes.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e7085	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! In my comments, I will briefly address the report by the Minister of Finance on the study carried out by the Austrian Institute for Economic Research on redistribution by public budgets in Austria. Austria has an above average state share in international comparison, but the redistribution efficiency of the tax system is low. The tax rate according to OECD definition is 43.5 percent. This means that we are in the ninth position of 24 OECD countries, or 2% above the average in the European Union. Considering the structure of taxes, it can be seen that income and income taxes amount to 26.9% in Austria and 38.8% in the OECD. The share of social insurance is 33 percent in Austria and 25 percent in the OECD, around one third in Austria and one quarter in the OECD. In order to develop the tax burden, it should be noted that after the economic downturn in 1975 there were increasingly difficulties in financing the social state. The burden reached its peak in the mid-1980s, was slightly reduced by the tax reform, and from 1990 the tax burden increased again. That, ladies and gentlemen, says this study. While the wage tax rate has increased significantly, the share of tax benefits has fallen sharply since 1974. In 1974 it was 20.2 percent, and at present this figure is 9 percent. With regard to the development of per capita income, it is noted that compared with the increase in gross pay per employee – it was 5.1 per cent between 1990 and 1994 – the ASVG-pensions increased by 4.1 per cent, but the public staff increased by 8.1 per cent. As mentioned above, the tax and tax rate is above the EU average. However, the redistributive effect of the taxes is relatively low, as the share of taxes with progressive effect – due to wage and income tax – is low in international comparison. In the last decade, the burden on regressive social security contributions and indirect taxes has shifted. Up to the maximum contribution limit, the social security contribution benefit is proportional to gross cover and is regressive. While the social tax rate for average income is about 18 per cent, it is only 6.6 per cent per month in relation to 100 000 S. The contributions to statutory social security cover a third of the tax revenue in Austria, as already mentioned, whereas the OECD averages only one quarter. The vertical redistribution by public-sector activities reaches a considerable extent. The upper third of households – without self-employed but with pensioners – account for 60 percent of gross household income, pay 62 percent of taxes and taxes, and receive 43 percent of public transfers. The average third has 12 per cent of gross household income, pays almost 10 per cent of all taxes, but receives 22 per cent of all transfers. These transfers account for 31 percent of their income for the lower third of households, only 12 percent for the upper third. The higher incomes in Austria benefit from the government's transfers per capita as much as the lower levels of incomes: However, they receive less than they pay, as measured by their tax benefits. In healthcare there is essentially the desired horizontal redistribution from healthy to sick. In the health sector, there is a clear redistribution of the family in which the woman is not employed and the children are in training for a long time. They do not pay any additional contributions, but are co-insuranced. Health expenditure is one of the top drivers in expenditure today. Overall health expenditure is already 10 percent of GDP, and the shift in the age structure of the population will increase the problem of health development, as health costs for older people are on average twice as high as for young people. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! If you read this study carefully, you can see some points that point to a third tax package of the government. Thus, the coordinator of this study criticises the abolition of property tax and believes that basic assets are massively undervalued by far too low unit values. This is what Dr. Guger – which is the coordinator of this study – wrote in a "Kurier" article on 1st October. ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e8898	Mrs Moser. — (DE) Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to speak on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party. Please, ladies and gentlemen. A voluntary time limit of 7 minutes is indicated.
ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e8919	Mr President. Federal Minister! Mr President of the Court of Auditors! High house! For millenniums, the Danube has been one of the most important traffic routes. The invention of the steam machine revolutionized the shipping on the Danube. This culminated in the founding of the DDSG in 1829. This foundation was certainly a decisive step towards the use of the Danube as a trade route with the most modern fleets of European inland waterways. Almost 170 years ago, the Donau-Dampfschiffahrts-Gesellschaft represented our Austria. But after the termination of passenger shipping and the silent liquidation of the DDSG donau travels, as decided by the Annual General Meeting last year, a great deal of Austrian history was lost. With one blow, the successes that our red and white ships had achieved under the flag of Austria on the Danube were forgotten. It was not only one of the oldest shipping companies in the world and also the first of the Danube, an integral part of the history of economics and technology in Austria, but also the oldest in Central and South-East Europe. As Austria has changed, so has society at that time. The fact that DDSG has been able to carry out its task for so long despite all crises and disasters and despite all the changes, not least thanks to the tens of thousands of employees who put their lives in the service of the DDSG and gave everything in faithful fulfilment of their duty until the bitter end. Thank you here! [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] If the government had set the necessary measures in time, perhaps the flag of the DDSG and thus the flag of Austria would be found on the Danube. ==References====External links== In December 1990, a division of the DDSG into a freight and passenger sector was considered necessary. Passenger shipping should be managed without federal subsidies as a profit-oriented tourist company, while the freight sector should be managed by the federal government from 1993 onwards without loss cover. The parent company's task now was to privatise the two subsidiaries as soon as possible. Although the DDSG as a parent company has thus fulfilled the contract given to it by the owner in the best possible way under the given circumstances, this approach was certainly not in the interest of maintaining Austrian inland waterway transport. The ship "Mozart" has been mentioned several times today, a permanent burner also in the media. It is not enough, ladies and gentlemen, on the other hand, that this loss carrier has been rejected. Although an Austrian entrepreneur was willing to pay a higher sum, the "Mozart" was transferred to a German ship owner who had been allowed to test this ship for even two years before paying, then submitted a defect list, a further discount from the first negotiated sale price of 120 million Schilling and, lastly, paid only more than 90 million schilling for it. [[Abg. Dr. Khol: 140 million schilling!]] The parent company's task now was, as I said, to privatize the two subsidiaries as soon as possible. The DDSG has fulfilled this in the case of passenger shipping under the circumstances as best as possible. However, the cargo ships were sold gifted instead. The same group, namely the Stinnes Group, which was still ready to pay for 600 million schilling for 49 percent of the company in 1989, then received the supplement by adding 1.6 billion schilling to it by the Austrian state – and that only for the DDSG-Cargo! Just as Semperit's production machines are being relocated to the Czech Republic because of lower production costs, the Stinnes Group, gift recipients of the DDSG-Cargo, has achieved the same purpose with the flagging of the ships in the former COMECON in order to operate with much cheaper nautical personnel. In both cases, Austrian jobs have been lost. It seems to me – and that has not yet been mentioned by any of my previous speakers – that a former DDSG employee, obviously in his attempt to take over the role of an "Economic Captain", has not only accounted for the questionable privatisation of the DDSG Cargo in the form of a gift to the Stinnes Group, but also the negative sales result. But all this is snow from yesterday. It is much more important now to look at the future of the DDSG remnant. Ladies and gentlemen, there is another need for attention. ==References====External links== ==References====External links==* Official website We still have old country rights and an old office building, and there are still twelve people in charge! We're supposed to need an exclusive board now! Accordingly, the tender has to be implemented by the company without creating a long-term basis of existence. I would therefore ask the Supervisory Board of the DDSG to reflect on the commercial meaning of this business concept before considering the successor of the Executive Board at the Supervisory Board meeting on 6 June. The question therefore arises: Was the DDSG renovated, privatized or even ruined by the long-standing spectacle of the unfavorable privatisation and the half-hearted solutions?

ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e9974	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! For our democracy in Austria, it is important that there is a system that is understandable to the public for political salaries. The reference of the President of the Federal Republic is accepted as the highest reference from a public office, for example a mayor of a 20 000 citizens' community would form the basis. In between, the references from all public functions, including self-government, are to be classified. This is an important objective, we insist on it, and we will keep our promise that we will implement a proposal in this regard and will decide in good time in this House. There are three political-related models. One model is the one we currently have: Linking salaries to a reference value, which in turn is linked to the reference in the public service. That is to say, the Federal President should earn a certain percentage more than what the highest official deserves, the percentage varies for holders of other functions. The other model is the all-in model, i.e. the politician is treated like a freelancer: He receives a fee, which is not subject to wage tax, but income tax, and so to speak, carries all the fees as a freelancer himself. The third model is the performance model, which assumes that a basic salary is paid, and depending on the role of the politician, i.e. if he is a committee chairman, scribe or a subcommittee, certain supplements are to be granted. Each of these models has advantages, each of these models has disadvantages. We have decided – and this is what the People's Party says – that we do not define our salary and the salary regime of the politicians themselves, but wait for the proposal of an independent Commission, at the head of which is a respected man with a respectable role in this Republic, namely the President of the Court of Auditors, who, beyond good and evil, coordinates and presents these proposals to us. I believe that we should, for good reason, wait for the Commission's proposals and not constantly hinder this Commission with intermediate calls or with good advice in its work from the gallery. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] We are patiently waiting for what this Commission is giving us. As I know the Court of Auditors' working capacity, I assume that we will have a proposal to that effect by the end of this year. That is why I and my group do not agree with the deadline of Mr Stadler, because I cannot take this seriously. We cannot use a Commission consisting of seven experts and a specialist, and then say: It's about a month now. We are waiting for this report, it will come by the end of the year. Ladies and gentlemen, something else: We have decided to abolish the so-called civil servant privilege, i.e. the unemployed income of officials who are also mandated, in July of this year under time pressure. I will use all I can to carefully examine the proposals made by the 'Pyramid Commission' and try to reach a consensus with all the parties in this House, because I believe that a pyramid of this kind should rest on a broad base. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] I therefore assume that we will have the proposals by the end of the year, that we will start discussions with the parties in January next year, and that, once we have successfully completed the discussions, we will come to a three-party, four-party or ideal five-party application, with no illusion: The last political arrangement, Mr Stadler, was decided by all the parties to this House, including the votes of the liberals.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e10481	Mr President. High house! The Court of Auditors has been given the task of reviewing the proper execution of the silent liquidation of the DDSG, and has presented to the National Council a report on this subject, which is today the subject of the debate. In its audit, the Court of Auditors found that the silent liquidation had been properly dealt with. In my opinion, however, it would not be possible to use it in this context in the context of this mere finding, which constitutes a summary of the entire report. At the same time, we must note that this silent liquidation has reached the end of a development which is unfortunate in several ways: regrettably for the DDSG itself, for it obviously cannot be built up for a company if it is still liquidated; Regrettably for all those who – and this has been done very aptly by Mr Edler – had jobs in the DDSG and in part lost these jobs; regrettably for tourism, for the tourism industry in the Danube, because it has lost a piece of Austria; regrets also because the Austrian flag on the Danube is now no longer represented to the extent that we would all wish; Finally, I regret that for the public sector and ultimately for the taxpayers who had to stand up for the fact that in recent years, during the last decades, many funds have been transferred to the DDSG, which we believe could have been used more effectively. When considering our report, it must not be ignored that this report begins as follows – and I may quote literally here: "As the Court of Auditors already pointed out in its 1994 Special Report, the Republic of Austria made significant financial sacrifices for the maintenance of the DDSG and the Donaurei GesmbH." – End of the quote. This is probably the most unfortunate finding to be made by the Court of Auditors in connection with the DDSG or the end of the DDSG. I cannot ignore the fact that this development did not come as a surprise to the Court of Auditors and that it could not be surprising for all those to whom the Court of Auditors presented the reports in the past. As regards the development of the DDSG, the Court of Auditors has warned several times and repeatedly and has already voted decades ago that consideration should be given to how the further fate of the DDSG should be designed. I just recall, for example, the reports of the Court of Auditors from 1978, 1984 and 1994. The recommendations and suggestions made by the Court of Auditors in these reports did not take account of the Court of Auditors' wishes and would have been desirable in the interest of the DDSG and also in the interest of taxpayers. It seems to me, therefore, on the occasion of this, as I have just said, that the last report by the Court of Auditors on the DDSG is appropriate to appeal to you, to the Members of the National Council, but also to the members of the Government. I appeal to the members of the National Council: Support the Court of Auditors if it makes recommendations because it depends on your support. The Court of Auditors has no executive power, it can only make recommendations, it can only give suggestions. It requires that his suggestions and recommendations be implemented by those who are obliged to report, and this is primarily the National Council. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ as well as the liberals.]] My appeal to those responsible in the government: Take the Court of Auditors' reports a little more seriously than has been done in the past in individual cases. The Court of Auditors shall draw up only decision-making bases. He himself does not make any decisions. He expects his recommendations to be addressed, and he expects his recommendations to be taken seriously. I think the case of DDSG is an exemplary one. It shows that it would have been appropriate to see in the past the consequences of the Court of Auditors. [[General condolences.]] I therefore believe that a positive consequence should be drawn from this in itself negative event, namely the end of the DDSG: Let us all avoid, together - the Court of Auditors, the Government and Parliament - that there will be further misdevelopments in the future such as those at the DDSG.

This concerns those provisions on the composition of the Habilitation Commissions, which the Constitutional Court has repealed. We have never shared the view of the Constitutional Court that only those with whom we can decide and judge whether anyone has a positive conclusion to the habilitation procedure that the Venia docendi themselves have, and we cannot share it with conviction in the future, because it is not about producing these opinions themselves, but it is about letting Members decide on existing opinions that are brought into the Habilitation Commission. We have clearly expressed a different view from that of the Constitutional Court. I think it is even a question of what is happening here, because in other bodies the question might also be asked: Can a councillor decide when it comes to building advice? Does he have to be a builder himself? - Only as an example. We must nevertheless take this into account, because the mandate of the Constitutional Court is clear. We will also take this into account, and we will take it into account to the extent that the Constitutional Court requires, in the form that the Habilitites are unable to agree on these decisions. A second point in the UOG-Novelle, which is hidden in a few lines, is the opening up, the possibility of Austrian universities to operate in other countries. The whole thing is based on the desire for a cooperation between Bolzano and Innsbruck. But this is also important for all universities. This can be equally important for Linz, Klagenfurt or Vienna. You meant we wouldn't take your objections into account here. We believe – because the proposal came from the liberals to change it – that that formulation, as we have provided, best takes into account the intentions. In the future, universities can also freely accept offers in other countries, taking into account costs. Finally, let me say a few words about the future priorities: I mentioned the UOG implementation. In reforming the right to study, we must also take greater account of the fact that there is a significant proportion – up to 40 percent of the report – of students who are either employed or even mainly employed. It is about a massive and necessary expansion of the high school sector. It's about getting more women to the university desks – and I'm going to say more to Col. Ablinger. And it is also about maintaining the university's open spaces even in times of scarceer resources. Especially in times of scarceer resources, good decision-making is particularly important so that you can make right decisions. This report provides such foundations to a very high extent. It will be our task to draw the relevant conclusions from it. We will be pleased to give our support to this report, which has been recognised by all the political groups in the Committee. Thank you.

On the one hand, the report offers a truly comprehensive and very well structured insight into the pay-, data- and fact-finding status quo in higher education. If you go through the detailed statistical part of a more detailed consideration, you will gain some surprising insight into the actual state of our universities. As an example, just take the list of the level of planning of the individual faculties for 1996. The Faculty of Medicine Vienna, including ordinary and extraordinary professors as well as assistants and scientific officials, has 1 766 planning posts on the one hand. On the other hand, the number of listeners in the same faculty in 1995/1996 was 10 957. This means that at the Faculty of Medicine there is actually a planning post for six students, especially for university assistants. At the Faculty of Medicine Innsbruck the ratio is even 5 : 1. I already know – my colleague, Mrs Gredler, is looking at me with a bit of a reproach – that in this number the staff of the university clinics with which medical care is covered is also included. Nevertheless, a ratio of 6 : 1 between students and university teachers certainly does not correspond to the dramatic situation described in connection with the savings package by the Faculty of Medicine in Vienna. In statistical terms, therefore, the higher education report provides a detailed analysis. It also provides information on events and behavioural results in recent years. For example, it also includes the state of implementation of the UOG or the development of the higher education sector. The reason why we do not give our overall support to this report is that the report does not go beyond the current state of affairs in much of an area. There is no honest discussion of the problem areas of higher education, nor will future developments, political projects and visions be addressed or implemented in the positive sense of the word. I have already raised this criticism in committee, which Dr Höllinger told me about, but there are some chapters in which future projects will be reported. As an example, he mentioned the subject of higher education in this context. Ladies and gentlemen! I admit that this is indeed a good example – however, an example that shows the shortcomings in this report regarding the presentation of developments and political projects. The presentation of Chapter 3.5 "Development of the sector by the year 2000" covers exactly a three-quarters page. Ladies and gentlemen! Read these five paragraphs on the further development of the subject area of higher education. There you will find empty formulas, non-binding statements and indications, but no honest discussion of the problem areas, such as the problem area between universities and the newly established high-school courses, nothing on the question of re-transmission of financial resources from the university to the university and no specific statements on the subject until when the spectrum of high-school studies is extended by the fields of social and health. The content of this chapter is fully clear if we compare it with the report of the University Council which was presented half a year ago. Ladies and gentlemen! Read the recommendation of the High School Council and, in particular, the supplement 21: Proposals for the further development of the high-school development plan. – None of the problem areas raised there, none of the proposals are taken up, discussed or even answered in the chapter of the higher education report on higher education. Ladies and gentlemen! Of course, the higher education report contains much more than the field of vocational colleges. I just wanted to point out to this example why we cannot give the report our overall support. And I'd like to repeat it again: This report makes it clear that the current state of the policy of the great coalition is a very unique one, namely the inability for political planning, but also the inability to present or discuss where the political journey is to go, which reforms, changes and objectives are to be sought. Instead, wherever there is only the slightest suspicion that any so-called acquired rights could be affected by any interest groups, one resorts to a position of political non-binding, which is hardly yet a starting point for an objective discussion. We all know that the current system will be difficult to finance in the future. We must therefore seriously consider whether the introduction of study fees on credit could be a sensible, balanced system for the future. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Especially in rural areas, the removal of free travel for students has led to an imbalance, an imbalance compared to the students in the urban area. While rural students have to purchase higher travel costs, students in the city can use cheap offers. Equality should be established in this respect. That is why I think it is fairer to introduce a model for equal opportunities. With the introduction of study fees on credit, students would have the opportunity to study unburdened and motivated. Reimbursement would be due only after completion of her studies. So the fees would not have to be paid immediately by the students, but would be advanced by a fund and should be reimbursed after the end of the studies as soon as the former student earns more than the average income. He deserves more because he was able to study, so he should pay it back. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] It would also ensure that poor-earning academics, unemployed or parents in Karenz are not yet burdened. I see this model as an additional incentive for performance, and the opportunities and opportunities would be more justly distributed. The Austrian People's Party stands for performance and equal opportunities. That is why we hope that others will look more closely at this model and win for it. The current system has not caused farmers' children and workers' children to have more and more come to universities. It is rather true that 50 percent of expenditure benefits the top third of income. We have a lot of drop-outs and a too long study duration. What costs is more appreciated, and we should proceed in accordance with this principle. In this context, however, I would also like to point out the importance of the colleges. Forcing this training system could lead to an upgrading of teaching. Parents are still shy about it, mainly because of the image problem of having their children teach. An improved permeability to the vocational colleges could make teaching attractive for young people again, and graduates of this training have good opportunities in the labour market. For Tyrol, I hope that more colleges will be built, as this means additional opportunities for well-founded and highly qualified training, especially for the districts far away from the university cities. That is why I am in favour – I will say this again and again – of the fact that Kufstein University of Applied Sciences is realized as soon as possible. To Mrs Motter: You're right. This report only strips the field of technical colleges, but we have recently discussed a very comprehensive high school report. I would like to say to Mr Niederweiser that I am no longer aware of this. I read he's for tuition fees, then he's not. – You best tell me that yourself; I would be interested in it. Mr Krüger says that he is against tuition fees, but as an opposition politician he will probably not be particularly concerned about financing in the future. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1996-10-31-020-XX-NRSITZ-00044_d7e13219	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! The security of the future of Austria lies in strengthening its competitiveness, and this strengthening of competitiveness can only be achieved through improved training. Only in this way can we maintain or improve the position of Austria in the international comparison. I am therefore particularly pleased that the positive development in the field of international relations, the opening and the cooperation between universities has been started. In this respect, a lot has been done during the reporting period. There are already a variety of scholarships and exchange programmes for studies and scientific work which can be used by Austrian students and teachers. Of course, Austria's students and teachers have more opportunities to participate in education programmes since EU accession in this area. In order to support participation in such programmes, our national care facility has been extended and re-structured, but the participation still does not take place to the extent I would like it to take place. For example, the programme for the training and mobility of scientists with the TMR key in Austria is still under-utilised – for example, in Action Line 2, this is access to large research facilities, not a single application from Austria or with Austrian participation. In general, however, it can be said that in the course of the 4th EU research programmes. Researcher and researchers from Austria were involved with over 1,400 project applications, and of these 1,400 project applications, some 300 were approved for EU funding. With a success rate of around 20 percent, Austria is on the European average. Just a few words about the possibilities of distance learning: I am very glad that we have introduced this with the university study law in 1995 at our universities. I hope that these opportunities will be extended and used accordingly. In this respect, there is a very cost-saving and resource-saving perspective for the future, especially for people living in rural areas or for professionals living in a place where there is no university. This would give such persons a better opportunity to study higher education. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! This report on higher education is generally very neat, well-designed, very clear. I would like to thank the officials of the Ministry for this position, and I would like to take note of this report on higher education. Thank you.
